        t -> next = u;

}
```

This is a complex loop body that:
1. When y is non-NULL: inserts node y between t and u, moves y forward, then moves t to u and u forward

Original:
```c
struct list * append(struct list * x, struct list * y)
/*@ With x_prev

```c
struct list * append(struct list * x, struct list * y)
/*@ With x_prev

struct list *multi_append(struct list *x, struct list *y, struct list *z)
/*@ Require dlistrep_shape(x,0) * dlistrep_shape(y,0) * dlistrep_shape(z,0)

OK so the `--ensure-emp` flag replaces `Ensure <anything> */` with `Ensure emp */`. Let me re-examine the original file:

```c
struct list * append(struct list * x, struct list * y)
/*@ With x_prev
    Require dlistrep_shape(x, x_prev) * dlistrep_shape(y,0)
  Ensure dlistrep_shape(__return,x_prev)
 */;

struct list *multi_append(struct list *x, struct list *y, struct list *z)
/*@ Require dlistrep_shape(x,0) * dlistrep_shape(y,0) * dlistrep_shape(z,0)
    Ensure  dlistrep_shape(__return,0)
 */
```

After `--ensure-emp`, both Ensures become `emp`. But `append` has `With x_prev`, which is an existential. The `* */` at the end should be captured by the regex. Let me check the regex: `r'Ensure\s+[^*]+?\*/'`. This matches `Ensure` followed by spaces, then one or more non-`*` characters (lazy), then `*/`. 

For `append`: `Ensure dlistrep_shape(__return,x_prev)\n */` → `Ensure  emp */`
For `multi_append`: `Ensure  dlistrep_shape(__return,0)\n */` → `Ensure  emp */`

That seems correct.

Let me try to understand why symexec is failing at the `append` call. Maybe the issue is that the Pre-loop state doesn't include a `With` clause for the `append(y, z)` call. The `multi_append` function has:
```
/*@ Require dlistrep_shape(x,0) * dlistrep_shape(y,0) * dlistrep_shape(z,0)
    Ensure  dlistrep_shape(__return,0)
 */
```

But the `append` function has:
```
/*@ With x_prev
    Require dlistrep_shape(x, x_prev) * dlistrep_shape(y,0)
  Ensure  emp */
```

Note: `append` has `With x_prev`, which means `x_prev` is an existential variable. When calling `append(y, z)`, symexec needs to find or instantiate `x_prev`. But `y` is a parameter of `multi_append`, and the `multi_append` function doesn't have a `With` clause for it. In the original require, `y` is part of `dlistrep_shape(y, 0)`, so `y->prev` should be 0.

But when the symexec tries to call `append(y, z)`, it needs to match `dlistrep_shape(y, y_prev) * dlistrep_shape(z, 0)` against the current heap state. The `y_prev` (or `x_prev` in the `append` signature) would need to be the prev of `y` in the current state.

At the call site (line 54/116):
- `y` might have been advanced during the loop. Some nodes of `y` were inserted into `x`.
- The remaining `y` list might be `dlistrep_shape(y_remaining, 0)` or `dlistrep_shape(y_remaining, prev_of_remaining)`.

Actually, I just realized something. Let me re-read the symexec error. The error is "with variable (id = 513) not instantiated". The "exist_mapping: 513 -> NULL" line at the end of the log shows that variable 513 maps to NULL. 

Maybe the problem isn't with the `append` call itself, but with some variable that should be instantiated earlier but isn't. The error location (line 116/54) might just be where symexec first notices the problem.

Looking at the unrolled code again, the issue might be in the loop body itself. When `y` is non-NULL, the code does:
```c
t -> next = y;
y -> prev = t;
t = y;
y = y -> next;
t -> next = u;
u -> prev = t;
if (y) {
    y -> prev = 0;
}
t = u;
u = u -> next;
```

Note: after `t = y` and `y = y->next`, `t->next` and `y->prev` are set to 0 (if y becomes NULL after advancing). But the issue might be that `t` is `y` (old), and `t->next` is being set to `u`. But `t` was just `y` (the old value), and `y->next` was the original next of y. After `y = y->next`, `t` points to the old y node. Setting `t->next = u` then severs the original link.

But wait, at the beginning of the loop iteration, we have:
- `t` pointing to some node in `x`
- `u = t->next` (the next node after `t` in `x`)
- `y` is a node in the y list

The loop body when y is non-NULL:
1. `t->next = y` — inserts y after t
2. `y->prev = t` — back-link y to t
3. `t = y` — move t to y
4. `y = y->next` — advance y
5. `t->next = u` — link t (which is y) to u
6. `u->prev = t` — back-link u to t
7. If `y` (new) is non-NULL: `y->prev = 0` — sever back-link of new y
8. `t = u` — move t to u
9. `u = u->next` — advance u

So effectively, it's inserting one node from the y list between t and u in the x list. Then it advances t past the inserted node to u, and advances u. y advances to the next node in the y list.

This is a complex operation. Maybe symexec can't handle this level of complexity due to internal limitations.

```c
while (u) {
    if (y) {
        t -> next = y;
        y -> prev = t;
        t = y;
        y = y -> next;
        t -> next = u;
        u -> prev = t;
        if (y) {
            y -> prev = 0;
        }
        t = u;
        u = u -> next;
    }
    else {
        u = append(u , z);
        t -> next = u;
        return x;   
    }
}
```

- `y` binds to `z`

**Analysis of the loop**:
- The loop takes nodes from y and inserts them between t and u in the x list.
- When y becomes NULL, it calls append(u, z) and returns x.
- When u becomes NULL, the loop exits.

The loop processes the x list. At each iteration:
- u points to some node in x (going forward)
- t points to u's predecessor in x
- y points to the next node to insert from y
- At the start, t = x, u = x->next

The pattern is:
- Before iteration: t points to a node in x, u = t->next points to next node, y is current head of remaining y list
- During iteration: insert y between t and u, advance y to y->next (with prev set to 0), advance t to u, advance u to u->next

- t becomes u (the old u)
- u becomes u->next (the old u->next)
- y becomes y_original->next (which was the next node in y)

So:
- t advances: t_next = u (one step forward in x)
- u advances: u_next = u->next (one step forward in x)
- y advances: y_next = y->next (one step forward in y)

But wait, y->next is changed during the body! Step 5: t->next = u. When we reach step 5, t is y (from step 3). So we set y->next = u (old u). So y's next pointer is now u, not the original next node in y.

Then step 4 already did y = y->next before step 5. Let me re-trace:

```
1. t -> next = y;    // t->next = y
2. y -> prev = t;    // y->prev = t
3. t = y;            // t = y
4. y = y -> next;    // y = y->next  (THIS happens BEFORE step 5, so y->next still points to original y list)
5. t -> next = u;    // t->next = u  (t is the original y node, so this changes y_original->next to u)
6. u -> prev = t;    // u->prev = t
7. if (y) { y -> prev = 0; }  // if y advanced to non-NULL, set its prev to 0
8. t = u;            // t = u
9. u = u -> next;    // u = u->next
```

So step 4 reads y->next BEFORE step 5 changes it. At step 4, y->next still points to the next node in the y list (because we haven't set y->next = u yet). So y advances to the next element in the y list.

After step 5, the original y node's next pointer is redirected to u.

This is correct: y advances within the y list, and the original y node is inserted into the x list.

Actually wait, looking more carefully: the pattern inserts y nodes after t, and t starts at x@pre, then advances. But the insertion always happens right after t. After:
- Iter1: insert y@pre after x@pre (t was x@pre), advance t to y_309
- Iter2: insert y_316 after y_309 (t was y_309), advance t to y_320

So the nodes are being inserted into x but NOT at the same position — they're being inserted right after the current t, and t advances each iteration.

- k nodes from y have been inserted into the x list
- All k inserted nodes are between x@pre's original position and the current u
- t is at position k after x@pre
- u is at position k+1 after x@pre
- y has advanced k nodes

Actually, I realize there's a deeper issue. Let me look at what the loop body actually needs:

The loop body (when u != 0 and y != 0):
```
t -> next = y;    // write to t->next
y -> prev = t;    // write to y->prev
t = y;            // just assignment
y = y -> next;    // READ y->next
t -> next = u;    // write to t->next (t is now old y)
u -> prev = t;    // write to u->prev
if (y) { y -> prev = 0; }  // write to y->prev (y is now old y->next)
t = u;            // assignment
u = u -> next;    // READ u->next
```

For these reads/writes, we need data_at:
- t->next (write) — owned by dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u) via unfolding
- y->prev (write) — owned by dlistrep_shape(y, 0) via unfolding
- y->next (read) — owned by dlistrep_shape(y, 0) via unfolding
- t->next (write, t is old y) — owned by dlistrep_shape(old_y, 0) via unfolding
- u->prev (write) — owned by dlistrep_shape(u, t) via unfolding
- y->prev (write, y is old y->next) — owned by dlistrep_shape(old_y_next, 0) via unfolding
- u->next (read) — owned by dlistrep_shape(u, t) via unfolding

So all the needed data_at are produced by unfolding the predicates in our invariant. 

But for unfolding to work, we need the node being dereferenced to be the first argument of a shape predicate, OR to be non-null so strategies can find the appropriate rule.

For dlistrep_shape(y, 0): if y != 0, strategies can unfold to expose y->next and y->prev. This is needed for lines 2-4. But the if(y) branch ensures y != 0 when these are executed.

For dlistrep_shape(u, t): if u != 0, strategies can unfold. Since we're inside the loop body (u != 0 from the condition), this should work.

For dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u): the segment might include t, but we need to unfold past all intermediate nodes to reach t. This requires that all intermediate nodes are non-null, which they should be in a valid DLL segment.

OK, I think the invariant is:
```c
/*@ Inv Assert x != 0,
    dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u) * dlistrep_shape(u, t) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0) * dlistrep_shape(z, 0)
 */
```

Actually, hmm. Looking again, I notice:
- `dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u)`: this means t is the last node of the segment, and t->next = u. But what if t->prev != 0?

Actually, the dllseg_shape definition says:
```
dllseg_shape(x, xp, yp, y) — DLL segment from x to y (y not included). x->prev = xp.
```

Where yp is the last node in the segment, and yp->next = y. yp IS the last node of the segment.

So dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u): x->prev = 0 (x is the head), the segment extends from x to t (including t), and t->next = u (u is NOT in the segment).

In RP_pre_loop:
- t = x@pre
- u = y_309
- dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, x@pre, y_309): segment from x@pre to x@pre, x@pre->prev = 0, x@pre->next = y_309
- dlistrep_shape(y_309, x@pre): rest of list from y_309

This works! Let me verify this invariant holds.

Let me also think about whether the `t` in dllseg_shape needs to be a field expression or an existential. Looking at the RP:
- pre_loop: dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, x@pre, y_309) → t = x@pre
- iter_2: dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, y_309, y_320) → t = y_309
- iter_3: dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, y_320, y_338) → t = y_320

In the invariant, t is the program variable, not the existential. So dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u) means:
- pre_loop: t = x@pre → dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, x@pre, y_309)
- iter_2: t = y_309 → dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, y_309, y_320)
- iter_3: t = y_320 → dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, y_320, y_338)

This works because t's value changes each iteration, and the dllseg_shape predicate re-evaluates with its current value.

Similarly, dlistrep_shape(u, t) means:
- pre_loop: u = y_309, t = x@pre → dlistrep_shape(y_309, x@pre)
- iter_2: u = y_320, t = y_309 → dlistrep_shape(y_320, y_309)
- iter_3: u = y_338, t = y_320 → dlistrep_shape(y_338, y_320)

These all match!

And dlistrep_shape(y, 0):
- pre_loop: y = y@pre → dlistrep_shape(y@pre, 0)
- iter_2: y = y_316 → dlistrep_shape(y_316, 0) [note: y_316->prev = 0 matches]
- iter_3: y = y_324 → dlistrep_shape(y_324, 0) [note: y_324->prev = 0 matches]

And dlistrep_shape(z, 0): same in all RPs.

So the invariant is:
```c
/*@ Inv Assert x != 0,
    dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u) * dlistrep_shape(u, t) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0) * dlistrep_shape(z, 0)
 */
```

But wait, should I also include something for `t` and `u`? Looking at what QCP does:
- t appears as argument 3 of dllseg_shape and argument 2 of dlistrep_shape
- u appears as argument 4 of dllseg_shape and argument 1 of dlistrep_shape

Both t and u are used in the loop body and appear in SEP. This should be fine.

Now, let me think about potential issues:

1. **dup_data_at**: The field expressions in the SEP predicates are `x`, `t`, `u`, `y`, `z` (program variables). There are no C field expressions like `t->prev`. So no dup_data_at from field expressions.

2. **Memory Read**: The loop body accesses `y->next`, `u->next`, `y->prev`, `u->prev`. These are exposed when dlistrep_shape(y, 0) and dlistrep_shape(u, t) are unfolded. The first argument of dlistrep_shape(y, 0) is y, so y->next and y->prev are directly accessible. Similarly for u->prev from dlistrep_shape(u, t).

3. **t->next**: This is accessed for writing. When we write to t->next, t might be in the dllseg segment (at entry). After t = y, t becomes part of dlistrep_shape(y, 0). After t = u, t becomes u and is in dlistrep_shape(u, t). The strategies should handle this.

Hmm, actually let me think about what happens step by step in the loop body with our invariant:

At loop entry:
State: dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u) * dlistrep_shape(u, t) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0) * dlistrep_shape(z, 0)

During the loop body (when y != 0):
1. `t -> next = y`:
   - t is in dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u). Since t is the last node of the segment, strategies can unfold dllseg_shape to the last node and expose t->next. Then we write y to t->next.
   - But this changes the endpoint of dllseg! After this, t->next = y, not u. So dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u) is no longer valid.

2. `y -> prev = t`:
   - y is in dlistrep_shape(y, 0). Unfold to expose y->prev, write t.
   - After this, y->prev = t.

3. `t = y`:
   - Assignment, changes the value of t.

4. `y = y -> next`:
   - y->next is exposed. Read it to get new y value.

5. `t -> next = u`:
   - t is now y_old. Its fields are exposed from dlistrep_shape(y_old, 0) (now mutated in step 2).
   - Write u to t->next.

6. `u -> prev = t`:
   - u is in dlistrep_shape(u, t_old). Unfold to expose u->prev, write new t.
   - But wait, u is in dlistrep_shape with prev = t_old. After writing, u->prev = new t (which is y_old).

7. `if (y) { y -> prev = 0; }`:
   - y is the new y (from step 4). Its prev is exposed. Write 0.

8. `t = u`:
   - Assignment, changes t value.

9. `u = u -> next`:
   - u->next is exposed. Read it.

After the body, the invariant should be re-established:
dllseg_shape(x, 0, t_new, u_new) * dlistrep_shape(u_new, t_new) * dlistrep_shape(y_new, 0) * dlistrep_shape(z, 0)

The strategies need to fold the modified state back into these predicates. This requires the appropriate fold rules in the strategies file.

I'm not 100% sure the strategies can handle all these mutations. The dllseq is being modified (extended by one node), and dlistrep shapes are being consumed and reformed. Let me try the invariant and see if verification passes.

"Assign Exec fail" at line 26:14 = `t -> next = y;`. This means the strategy can't handle the assignment. The `t->next` field can't be written to because there's no valid `data_at` for it.

This is a "Memory Read/Write" issue. The invariant doesn't expose `t->next` for the loop body.

From SKILL.md:
- **Cause 1 — Variable not in invariant**: The invariant replaces the program state. If t is not in the invariant, its binding is lost.
- **Cause 2 — Variable in invariant but no unfold path**: The field being accessed is not exposed. Strategies can only unfold from the **first argument** of shape predicates.

In our invariant:
```

Now I can see the strategy rules. Let me analyze the key ones:

**Rule 5** (core 6): Unfold dlistrep_shape from the LEFT
- Requires: dlistrep_shape(p, prev) at 0 + (p != NULL) at 1
- Action: erase dlistrep_shape, add data_at for p->next, p->prev, and dlistrep_shape of rest

**Rule 6** (core 6): Unfold dlistrep_shape from the RIGHT
- Requires: (p != NULL) at 0 + dlistrep_shape(p, prev) at 1
- Action: erase dlistrep_shape, add data_at for p->next, p->prev, and dlistrep_shape of rest

**Rule 7** (core 3): Combine dllseg ending at p with dlistrep starting at p
- Left: dllseg_shape(p, q, r, s) at 0
- Right: dlistrep_shape(p, x) at 1
- Check: infer(q == x) (i.e., the prev of p from the segment equals the prev of p from dlistrep)
- Action: erase both, add dlistrep_shape(s, r) (full list from s to r)

**Rule 11** (core 5): Erase empty dllseg (right side)
- Right: dllseg_shape(p, q, r, s) at 0
- Check: infer(q == r) && infer(p == s) (empty segment)
- Action: erase

**Rule 13** (core 3): Extend dllseg from the right (contract from left)
- Right: dllseg_shape(p, q, s, m) at 1
- Left: data_at(field_addr(s, list, next), ..., n) at 2
- Check: infer(m == n) && infer(s != 0)
- Action: erase dllseg, add data_at for s->next(=m) and s->prev(=y, existential), add dllseg_shape(p, q, y, s)

**Rule 14** (core 3): Contract dllseg from the right (rightmost node)
- Left: dllseg_shape(p, q, s, m) at 0
- Right: data_at(field_addr(s, list, prev), ..., n) at 1
- Check: infer(m != p) (segment not empty)
- Action: erase dllseg, add data_at for s->next(=m) and s->prev(=y, existential), add dllseg_shape(p, q, y, s) (but the last node is now y instead of s), add s != 0

**Rule 16** (core 3): Contract dllseg from the left
- Left: dllseg_shape(p, q, s, m) at 1
- Right: data_at(field_addr(s, list, next), ..., n) at 2
- Check: infer(s != q)
- Action: brings one node from the right into dllseg from the left

4. y = y->next: y advances
5. t->next = u: reconnects t (now y_old) to u
6. u->prev = t

8. t = u: t moves to u
9. u = u->next: u advances

- y = old_y->next
- One node from y has been inserted between old_t and old_u

- The segment from x to old_t has grown by one node (old_y)

Actually, looking at the loop body again:
1. t->next = y: breaks the boundary (t->next was u, now it's y)
2. y->prev = t: corrects y's prev
3. t = y: t becomes y (shifts the cursor)
4. y = y->next: y advances
5. t->next = u: reconnects t (now y_old) to u
6. u->prev = t: corrects u's prev
7. if y: y->prev = 0: severs old y's prev
8. t = u: t becomes u

The segment has grown: it used to end at t_old, now it ends at u_old. The extra node between t_old and u_old is y_old.

But from the invariant's perspective: before, t points to t_old and u points to u_old. After, t points to u_old and u points to u_old->next. The invariant just needs to hold with the new values of t and u.

`dllseg(x, 0, t_new, u_new) = dllseg(x, 0, u_old, u_old->next)` — this segment includes nodes from x to u_old (which includes the inserted y_old node). `dlistrep(u_new, t_new) = dlistrep(u_old->next, u_old)` — rest of list from u_old->next.

So the invariant IS preserved after the loop body. The question is whether the strategies can VERIFY this preservation.

The issue with the "Assign Exec fail" is that symexec can't even EXECUTE the assignment. This is earlier than strategy verification. The assignment itself needs the state to have data_at for t->next.

In the invariant state, dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u) contains t and its fields, but they're folded inside the predicate. For the assignment to execute, QCP needs to either:
1. Unfold the predicate first (using strategies), OR
2. The predicate itself should directly allow field access

Option 1 requires strategies to fire BEFORE the assignment. But strategies fire during entailment checking, not during program execution.

Actually, in QCP's execution model, when the program state is given by an assertion (the invariant), the state IS the assertion. To execute `t->next = y`, QCP needs `data_at` for t->next to exist in the state. If it's inside a folded predicate, QCP can't just reach in and modify it.

So the strategies need to fire BEFORE the assignment to unfold the predicate. But when do strategies fire?

In QCP, strategies fire during "assertion normalization" or "state simplification". When entering the loop body, the invariant state is "normalized" by applying strategy rules. This should unfold predicates as needed.

But if no strategy rule applies (as we analyzed — rule 14 needs data_at for t->prev which we don't have), the state stays as folded predicates, and the assignment fails.

```

`at 0` and `at 1` specify the positions in the assertion. They're NOT flexible — they specifically match the item at that index.

Actually wait, I just realized something. The issue might not be about unfolding strategies at all. Maybe QCP handles field assignments differently.

In QCP, when you write `t->next = y`, QCP needs:
1. The value of t (pointer)
2. Compute t->next's address (&(t->next))
3. A data_at for that address to have write permission

For (1), t's value comes from its binding in the invariant (as predicate arg).
For (2), &(t->next) is computed from t's value.
For (3), the data_at for t->next should be provided by the invariant (through predicate unfolding).

But QCP might need the data_at to ALREADY BE in the state (not folded in a predicate). If the state only has folded predicates, QCP can't "see" the data_at to grant permission.

If this is the issue, then I need to EITHER:
A) Structure the invariant so data_at for t->next is explicit, OR
B) Trigger strategy unfolding before QCP tries the assignment

For (A): I could write `dlistrep_shape(t->next, t)` instead of `dlistrep_shape(u, t)`. But this creates t->next as a field expression (causing dup_data_at with dllseg).

For (B): Strategies fire during entailment, not during execution. But maybe there are "pre-assignment" strategies.

Actually, I think in QCP, when the state is an assertion (the invariant), and a command (assignment) is executed, the system uses WEAKEST PRECONDITION or STRONGEST POSTCONDITION reasoning. For `t->next = y` with invariant as state, it might:
1. Check that t->next exists in the state (some data_at for &(t->next))
2. Modify the data_at to point to y
3. Update the state

If t->next doesn't exist in the state (because it's inside a folded predicate), step 1 fails.

So either the invariant needs to explicitly include data_at for t->next, or strategies need to be triggered to unfold before the assignment.

- dllseg_shape(x, 0, t->prev, t): uses t->prev as yp (last node) AND creates data_at for the FIELD t->prev → might create data_at(&(t->prev), ..., addr_of_t_prev)
- dlistrep_shape(t->next, t): uses t->next as l (head) AND creates data_at for the FIELD t->next → might create data_at(&(t->next), ..., addr_of_t_next)

dllseg also uses t as endpoint (no data_at for t). dlistrep also uses t as prev (value, no data_at).

The data_at for t->prev (from dllseg) and t->next (from dlistrep) are different fields at different addresses. No dup_data_at!

But are both t->prev and t->next non-null in the loop? At entry: t = x@pre, t->prev = 0 (NULL). dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, 0, x@pre) would be emp. dlistrep_shape(t->next, t) = dlistrep_shape(x@pre->next, x@pre) covers the rest.

But data_at(&(t->prev), ..., 0) for t = x@pre: t->prev = 0. This is just a field value. No node at address 0.

For t->prev in dllseg as yp: this is 0, so the segment from x@pre to 0. dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, 0, x@pre): x == y && xp == yp (x@pre == x@pre && 0 == 0) → emp. Good, empty segment.

For t->prev in dlistrep for the prev param: t->prev = 0. This is the prev value of the head node.

For t->next as l in dlistrep: t->next = u (initial value). dlistrep_shape(u, t) = dlistrep_shape(t->next, t): covers from u with prev = t.

So the invariant is:
```
dllseg_shape(x, 0, t->prev, t) * dlistrep_shape(t->next, t) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0) * dlistrep_shape(z, 0)
```

But `u` is NOT in this invariant! The loop condition `while(u)` would fail because u is not bound.

Unless... QCP binds variables from field expressions? If the invariant says `dlistrep_shape(t->next, t)`, and from the pre-loop code QCP knows `u = t->next`, maybe QCP resolves u to t->next?

I doubt this works. QCP needs explicit bindings.

OK, I've been going back and forth. Let me just try a practical approach: test a few invariants and see which ones compile. I'll start with the cleanest that includes both t and u as predicate args:

```
dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u) * dlistrep_shape(u, t) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0) * dlistrep_shape(z, 0)
```

And try to fix the "Assign Exec fail" by working on strategies.

dlistrep_shape(u, t_prev): head = u, u->prev = t_prev

Where is t? t is NOT in this invariant. The loop body accesses t->next, which would fail.

Hmm, what if I make the split at t AND include u:
dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u): ends at t, t->next = u
dlistrep_shape(u, t): head = u, u->prev = t

This is the original. And t->next = u. The invariant says this. But strategies can't unfold dllseg to expose t->next.

I think this is genuinely impossible with the current strategies. Let me output the result.

The invariant `dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u) * dlistrep_shape(u, t) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0) * dlistrep_shape(z, 0)` is the best one I derived. It's structurally correct:
- It matches all 3 recording points (RP_pre_loop, RP_iter_2, RP_iter_3)
- It binds all necessary program variables (x, t, u, y, z)
- The dllseg + dlistrep split correctly models the traversal cursor (t) and the remaining list (from u)

The verification fails because the first operation in the loop body is `t->next = y`, which writes to a field of the last node in dllseg_shape. The QCP strategies don't have a rule to unfold dllseg from the right to expose t->next's data_at. The `p = p->next` example in SKILL.md works because p is at arg[0] of dlistrep_shape, which CAN be unfolded, but in our case t is deep inside dllseg_shape and can't be reached.

I've tried numerous approaches:
1. Putting t at arg[0] via dllseg_shape(x, 0, t_prev, t) * dlistrep_shape(t, t_prev) — but then u is missing
2. Adding has_permission(&u) — causes segfault with exists syntax
3. Field expressions (t->prev, t->next) — would bind t but not u as program variable
4. Dlistrep-only approach — same "Assign Exec fail"
5. Various orderings of predicates — no change

This is a fundamental limitation of the current strategies.

## Task Summary

## Workflow Executed

### Step 1: Symbolic Execution & State Collection
1. **Unrolled** the loop via `unroll_loop.py --ensure-emp --depth 3` to `/workspace/multi_append_unrolled.c`
2. **Ran symexec** on the unrolled file (despite a late fatal error at the post-loop `append` call, recording points were generated)
3. **Parsed** the log with `qcp_parse_log.py`, successfully extracting 3 recording points:
   - `RP_pre_loop`: Before first iteration (t=x, u=x->next)
   - `RP_iter_2`: After one iteration (t advanced to y_309, u to y_320, y to y_316)
   - `RP_iter_3`: After two iterations (t to y_320, u to y_338, y to y_324)

### Step 2: Invariant Inference
Analyzed the loop's purpose: inserts nodes from the `y` list between `t` and `u` in the `x` list. The invariant derived from diff analysis is:

```c
/*@ Inv Assert
    dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u) * dlistrep_shape(u, t) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0) * dlistrep_shape(z, 0)
 */
```

This is structurally correct -- it matches all 3 recording points and binds all necessary program variables.

### Step 3: Verification Failure
The symexec verification fails with `Assign Exec fail` at `t->next = y` (the first statement in the loop body). The root cause: the invariant places `t` as the last node inside `dllseg_shape(x, 0, t, u)`, folded. QCP strategies have no rule to unfold `dllseg_shape` from the right to expose `t->next`'s `data_at` for the write operation. This differs from the standard forward-traversal pattern (`p = p->next`) where the cursor is at `arg[0]` of `dlistrep_shape` and strategies can unfold it directly.

Multiple retry attempts were made with different invariant structures (moving `t` to `arg[0]` via `exists t_prev` + `dlistrep_shape(t, t_prev)`, field expressions, etc.), but each introduced other issues: missing variable bindings (`u`), `has_permission(&u)` segfaults, or predicate overlap. No structure within the strategy capabilities could expose `t->next` for the write while also binding all required program variables.
